@@3949829 <p> As we emerge , temporarily perhaps , from weeks of the coldest weather since records began -- with snow disasters right round the northern hemisphere , from the US and Europe to China and Mongolia -- more examples come to light of how the cost of extreme cold is far greater than that of warming . We already have a ? 9.6 billion backlog to cover repairs to roads damaged in previous winters , and the price of repairing the potholes and crumbling asphalt caused by this winter 's even more intense cold threatens to raise that by billions more ( even though Government cuts will trim that budget by 15 per cent ) . <p> In Northern Ireland , 80,000 households were deprived of water by burst mains operated by the state-owned Northern Ireland Water . Yet as recently as October it published a strategic plan wholly obsessed by the need to transform its infrastructure to meet the challenge of global warming . <p> Another frozen chicken which came home to roost was the crisis confronting many of the eight million homes now heated by condensing @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ a way of reducing Britain 's carbon footprint . What Mr Prescott failed to do was impose , on this tightly regulated industry , any requirement that the pipes to take away the resulting water should not be placed on the outside of buildings . ( Responsibility for this crucial system failure has since been passed to the Health and Safety Executive . ) <p> The result is that up to a million external pipes froze in December , shutting off the heating . @@3949908 <h> What can David Cameron learn from Margaret Thatcher ? <p> In the week that papers from Margaret Thatcher 's first year in power were made public , Tim Montgomerie reflects on the Iron Lady 's continuing influence . <p> Why does Thatcher still matter ? <p> The grocer 's daughter from Grantham was n't just the first woman prime minister . She won three elections , rescued Britain from economic decline and tamed the unions that had brought previous governments to their knees . In winning the Falklands War , she restored Britain 's military standing . Her alliance with President Reagan helped defeat the enemy within ( the unilateralist disarmament movement ) and the enemy without ( the Soviet Union ) . Even today , 20 years after she was brutally removed by her own party , Lady Thatcher is among the most recognised symbols of Britishness . <p> So she 's the perfect role model for David Cameron ? <p> Some Tories certainly think so -- but over time , people have become very selective in how they remember both Thatcher and Thatcherism . Like @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ a giant elephant , Conservatives focus on different aspects of her legacy . Some , with their hands on the tough hide , revere her unbending will . Those near the swishing tail recall her tactical flexibility and the fact that she did very little about education or the NHS until very late . Others , drenched in water from the trunk , obsess about the poll tax and the sense that she was indifferent to the plight of the poorest . <p> In the early days , he said little about her , and chose not to be seen with her . That changed in 2007 , when Gordon Brown invited the Baroness to Downing Street : the new Prime Minister wanted to piggyback on her reputation for straight talking as part of his fruitless attempt to distance himself from Tony Blair 's years of spin . Since that time , Cameron has realised the potency of the Thatcher brand : in fact , she was one of his first guests at Number 10 . <p> How would she tackle the deficit ? <p> Thatcher 's focus was runaway inflation @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ was less tough on spending than Cameron promises to be : as opposition leader , she accepted expensive pay settlements and , once elected , she had the advantage that Labour , under orders from the IMF , had already introduced austerity measures . Although Britain 's economy is much stronger than when Thatcher came to power , the current public finances will be far tougher to fix . <p> And how did she get the economy growing ? <p> Thatcher and Cameron 's approaches to public spending might not be that dissimilar -- but they have very different attitudes towards boosting growth . Like Cameron , Thatcher increased VAT , but she also cut the higher rates of income tax that had produced a brain drain of talent . Cameron has opted to keep the 50p top rate of tax . Thatcher abolished the exchange controls that hindered international trade and began a steady programme of privatisation , deregulation and trade union reform , transforming Britain from the sick man of Europe into its fastest-growing economy . It was micro-economic genius from Thatcher that more than compensated for some macro-economic @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ been so bold on the deficit , displays no similar urgency : burdens on banks and climate change policies actually endanger competitiveness . <p> What else can Cameron learn ? <p> Francis Maude , the head of the Cabinet Office , has claimed that Cameronism promises to be bolder than Thatcherism . He 's right : the ambition of the " breakneck Coalition " is extraordinary . Thatcher preferred to under-promise and over-deliver . She also prized great consistency : her insistence in 1980 that " the Lady 's not for turning " cemented her reputation as the woman to end the vacillation that had bedevilled preceding governments . Cameron , by contrast , has already executed a number of about-turns . Some , on knife crime and Europe , can be blamed on the Coalition Agreement . Others , on school sports and funding of free books for low-income families , have been unforced errors . <p> And what about Europe ? <p> Over time , she became more Eurosceptic . Admirers remember the successful rebate battle of 1984 , won after some serious swinging of her handbag -- but @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ new powers . Like his predecessor , Cameron has fought to limit Britain 's contribution to the EU budget , but he uses his Coalition with the EU-friendly Lib Dems as a reason for not seeking the promised repatriation of powers . <p> And what can he learn from her mistakes ? <p> Thatcher was right to dismiss those Macmillanites who urged her to stick with the post-war consensus . But in her later years , that attitude mutated into a deafness that served her badly . Cameron needs to listen to a wide variety of voices , which means opening up his small clique of advisers . He has already learnt from Thatcher 's biggest weakness : although her memoirs talked about a " Social Thatcherism " , she did not build a balanced Conservatism when in power . Cameron has made social justice and environmentalism part of his programme from day one .

@@3950412 <p> Two glaciers sit almost side by side in one of the wildest and most starkly beautiful regions of Argentina . The Upsala is melting rapidly , but Perito Moreno , less than 40 miles away and part of the same ice field , is stable -- if anything , it has advanced steadily over the past century . The glaciers present a puzzle . One that I had come to Patagonia to try to unravel . <p> " That 's the trouble with Nature , " said my guide , Claudia Guerrido . " There are rules , but Nature does n't always play by the rules . " Claudia is a botanist . She 's about to do a doctorate on tree rings , looking into what ancient forests in the area can tell us about the long-term climate . <p> We were in the deepest south of Argentine Patagonia , heading out of the little town of El Calafate , beside Lago Argentina -- the lake into which both glaciers feed . Across the lake , mountains rose abruptly to picture-book points , frosted with @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and dry clump-grass of the Patagonian Steppe , the largest desert in the Americas . <h> Related Articles <p> It was nearing sundown when I arrived . The glaciers would have to wait for the morning . Meanwhile , I took a walk through the scrub that sloped gently in front of the hotel to a patch of wetland . <p> Flamingos -- birds I had thought belonged in warmer climes -- stalked the shallow water ; beside them were swans , a rare breed with white bodies but black heads and necks . A pair of Andean condors circled overhead , glided effortlessly on their awe-inspiring 10ft wings towards the mountains . Hares hopped unconcerned between clusters of hard , yellow grass . <p> In the dying light , the sky -- dappled with cloud -- became a watercolour wash of more shades of soft blue and gentle grey than I had ever thought possible . It took the faint strains of a tango from the hotel bar to remind me that I was in Patagonia , not some corner of Paradise -- that and the bleached skeletons of cattle @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , even for farm animals . But that night I went to sleep with the curtains open . I had never before looked forward to waking at dawn . <p> Claudia was there early , for the short drive to the Perito Moreno glacier . Keeping an eye open for wild guanacos ( similar to llamas ) and rhea , we left the dry steppe and were soon winding up through the mountain forests of Los Glaciares National Park . Here , a series of balconies and walkways is cut into a cliff fronting a tongue of the glacier , where it meets the waters of a fjord . <p> " It 's as if the landscape were made to view the glacier , " said Claudia , and indeed , the 200ft-high wall of ice is just a few hundred yards from the cliff face . <p> Over the past few decades , the approaching ice has , from time to time , met and frozen to the cliff face . Slowly , the waters of the lake erode it from beneath , forming an arch , and pressure builds @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the ice-bridge collapses , and the slow process begins over again . <p> This first happened in 1980 , then in 1988 , then in quick succession in 2004 , 2006 and 2008 . Some say this shows the glacier is speeding up its advance ; others relate the change in behaviour to global warming , or say that the Perito Moreno is atypical , fed by a larger snow catchment than other glaciers . <p> The ice wall seemed close enough to touch , jagged and fissured in places , pockmarked in others , at times white , at times ( where the ice was oldest and densest ) a surreal blue . <p> Here and there it was streaked with brown , where the glacier had crushed and devoured boulders in its path . It seemed very much alive . <p> Suddenly a crack like a gunshot would ring , or we 'd hear a deep thunderous rumble and fearsome roar , and part of the ice face , sometimes the size of a skyscraper , would shudder and crash into the lake . <p> " But that 's @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ warming , " Claudia pointed out . " It happens because Perito Moreno touches the bedrock . There is nowhere else for the ice to go when the lake water melts it . " <p> We made our way to the lake shore . A boat took me across to a rocky beach , where a guide was waiting to lead a group of us on a trek across the ice . For the next couple of hours , boots in crampons , obeying instructions to waddle like penguins up inclines and stomp straight-footed on the way down , we walked on the glacier . <p> Up close , the ice surface sparkled with tiny crystals , broken by deep turquoise shafts and pools formed by the action of dripping water . We climbed steep slopes and descended into crevices , returning exhilarated and exhausted . <p> The approach to the Upsala glacier , next day , was more sedate -- in a boat up a branch of Lago Argentino . Unlike Perito Moreno , the Upsala glacier sits on a layer of water . Here there are fewer dramatic cracks @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ icebergs break off and float away -- but they are doing so at such a rate that they have completely blocked the entrance to the fjord leading to the glacier . <p> We moored at a former estancia nearby , and a park ranger took us in a 4WD through a moonscape terrain to a mountaintop viewpoint . It overlooked a vast valley now deserted by the glacier , which is withdrawing at a rate of 650ft a year . But the ranger was adamant that the Upsala 's retreat had to do with local geological and environmental conditions , not climate change . I 'm not sure I was convinced -- there are so many other glaciers in rapid retreat . But then , there was the stable Perito Moreno , just over the hill . <p> Discussion of these issues can grow heated , and in the end I do n't think I did manage to solve the puzzle I had come to unravel . But one thing I was sure of -- that I had just been in one of the most intoxicatingly beautiful spots on Earth .

@@3951325 <p> Trisha Hollingworth knew that it was time to evacuate her home on the outskirts of Rockhampton when she awoke to floodwaters lapping at the front steps . She had been told that the water was coming , but the speed with which it arrived took her by surprise . The front garden , where she had celebrated New Year 's Eve with a barbecue with her neighbours , was one foot under water by the morning of January 1 . Two days later , the rust-coloured water had reached the fifth step of her home , just inches shy of her front door . It was time to go . <p> By then , the only way out was in the " tinnie " -- a small aluminium boat usually reserved for fishing on the nearby Fitzroy River . But ever since the river burst its banks , the boat has replaced the family car . " You have to boat in and boat out now . If you tried to walk , the water would be up to your chest and it is running so fast @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ says . " The road has become part of the river , it is like living in the middle of the ocean . " <p> Rockhampton , a town of 77,000 people 370 miles north of Brisbane , lies close to the coast , on the Fitzroy , one of Australia 's largest river systems . It is among the biggest of the 22 towns affected by the tide of flooding that has swept across the eastern Australian state of Queensland over the past three days , and also one of the worst hit . <p> All main routes to the south , north and west of the city have been cut by the rising water . Rail lines and the airport runways are also submerged and the floodwaters stretch for several miles in each direction . On the Bruce Highway to the south , one of the few things to rise above the water is the " Welcome to Rockhampton " sign . <p> " Rockhampton is an island , " the town 's mayor , Brad Carter , says . " You can look down a street for a @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ as the human residents , the local wildlife has been caught off-guard by nature 's excesses . Bewildered and hungry kangaroos can be seen gathered together on any scrap of high ground and at night the sound of bellowing cattle , lost and confused by the water , fills the air . <h> Related Articles <p> And the water is still rising . Floodwaters stand at about 30ft and over the next 48 hours the Fitzroy River is expected to swell another 15in , to levels not seen since 1954 . At that height , an estimated 1,400 homes in Rockhampton will be inundated . In an attempt to prevent any more deaths or serious injuries in the floodwater , forced evacuations are under way across the town , with residents directed to emergency shelters where they can expect to spend at least a week waiting for the waters to subside . <p> Mrs Hollingworth and her husband , Frank , decided to evacuate to a caravan in the centre of the town . Their neighbour Lee Carol chose to join them after she realised that her low-lying home would be @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ put the chooks chickens and the turkeys on the roof and the birds on the kitchen table , but I 'll lose everything else , " she says . Speaking last night from the bar of the Great Western Hotel , where many Rockhampton residents had gathered to swap stories about the flood , she said she was desperately worried about her property , " but there is nothing I can do to save it . " Miss Carol said she wanted to stay at the house for as long as possible , but was put off by the arrival of scores of deadly brown snakes . " I shot 17 in one day , and a woman down the road shot 26 , " she says . " They come out of the water looking for dry ground all day , but they can kill you just as quick as the floods can . " <p> The Hollingworths and Miss Carol , who live in the Rockhampton suburb of Port Curtis , are just a few of the 200,000 people displaced by the Queensland floods that have submerged an area @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ towns , mines and farms into a muddy inland sea and killed three people so far . Two of those who died had been trying to flee the floodwaters in their cars when they were swept away . The floods -- triggered by torrential rain which fell on the Sunshine State before Christmas -- have been blamed on the La Ni ? a weather pattern , during which the tropical seas of the Pacific Ocean cool , prompting heavy rains and storms over the east coast of Australia . <p> The floods came just months after the end of a 10-year drought and two years after bush fires that killed more than 170 people in the southern state of Victoria . But farmers who had been used to praying for rain are not celebrating , for these floods are devastating . <p> The storms , cyclones and flooding are the latest examples of extreme weather to hit Australia , the driest inhabited continent on Earth , which is considered to be highly vulnerable to climate change . While Australians are the largest per capita carbon emitters on the globe , political @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ year after the then prime minister , Kevin Rudd , failed to push his carbon trading scheme through parliament . His successor , Julia Gillard , has since pledged to put a price on carbon , but faces strong opposition from Tony Abbott , the leader of the conservative Liberal-National coalition , who once said that climate change was " absolute crap " . However , the soaring cost of the floods -- already estimated at $1 billion -- could restart the discussion . <p> The rising waters have washed away vital rail tracks linking mines to the ports , halting operations at 75 per cent of Queensland 's coal fields and forcing some miners to suspend production and cancel shipments . Australia is the world 's largest exporter of coal , most of which comes from mines in Queensland . With no coal to load , the crisis has left a backlog of empty freighters along the nation 's busiest shipping lanes , delaying deliveries to Europe and Asia . <p> The state 's wheat and sugar industries have also been affected , as has the tourist industry , which @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ summer months . Anna Bligh , the Queensland state premier , has warned that she expects the state to face serious long-term effects from the natural disaster . And Miss Gillard said recovery would take " a significant amount of time " . <p> But while people in some parts of the state start the process of returning home and surveying the damage , the authorities have warned that this crisis " of biblical proportions " is far from over . Floodwaters from the north of Queensland are yet to make their way into several low-lying towns , and forecasters are predicting yet more rain , more catastrophic flooding and more loss of life . <p> Warren Bridson , acting assistant director-general of Emergency Management Queensland , said that the authorities were " very conscious " of the likelihood that more wet weather was on the cards . " The Bureau of Meteorology predicted this to be a very severe wet season , " he says . " We still have three months ahead of us , so we must expect lots more of what we 're currently having . " @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ this season . " We 've had one , " Mr Bridson says . " Theoretically we have four to go . " Queensland could n't ask for a worse prediction .

@@3955863 <h> Gulf of Mexico oil leak may give Arctic climate clues <p> Image caption Vast amounts of methane are present in the Arctic , often in the form of hydrates <p> Almost all of the methane released in the Gulf of Mexico oil leak was quickly swallowed by bacteria - which may give clues to climate change in the Arctic . <p> Writing in journal Science , US researchers report that methane-absorbing bacteria multiplied in the Gulf following the April accident . <p> The Arctic contains vast stores of methane , and its release could quickly accelerate warming around the world . <p> But scientists caution that the regions are very different . <p> The research ship Pisces , operated by the National Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration ( Noaa ) , made several voyages into the Gulf in the months following the leak . <p> " There were thousands of different types of molecule coming out of the well , and by far the most abundant was methane , " said research leader John Kessler , from Texas A and M University . <p> " In fact , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ " Normally we have to go around and study what 's seeping naturally out of the sea floor ; but this release was massive and localised . " <p> Virtually all of the methane remained suspended and dissolved in sea water at depths of around one kilometre . <p> Bacteria in the water that have evolved to process naturally-seeping methane apparently increased in number , taking oxygen from the water as they did so , and mopping up the Deepwater Horizon 's cargo . <p> The team 's calculations indicate that less than one hundred thousandth of the gas released made it into the atmosphere . <h> Warming warning <p> Methane is the second most important gaseous component of the man-made portion of the greenhouse effect . <p> Image caption The Gulf waters were sampled for methane <p> And vast quantities of it - no-one knows how much , though some estimates put it as 3,000 times the current amount in the atmosphere - lie trapped in the sea bed around the Arctic . There are other deposits around the world as well . <p> Some see this methane hydrate as @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ if it escapes as the seas warm , it will act to amplify global warming . <p> In principle , the Deepwater Horizon research could help inform scientists about how serious methane releases could be - how much would be processed in sea water , and how much would make it into the atmosphere . <p> We do n't know the distribution of the methane hydrates - we do n't know how pervasive they are in the regionProfessor Graham Westbrook , Birmingham University <p> " In the Arctic , there definitely would be some sort of microbial response ; and in most places on the sea floor that have the capacity for massive release , there will be some seepage that will help to populate the water with microbes that could respond , " Dr Kessler told BBC News . <p> However , he cautioned , there are big differences between the Gulf of Mexico leak and potential methane sources in the Arctic . <p> Ed Dlugokencky , who runs Noaa 's global registry of methane concentrations but was not involved in the Gulf research , agreed . <p> " The @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ likely only applies to deep waters , not areas where hydrates exist on shallow continental shelves such as the East Siberian Arctic shelf , " he said . <p> " There , water is only about 40m deep , and bubbling is an important mechanism for transport of methane from the sediments to the atmosphere . " <p> The shallower the water , the less time methane would spend in it , making absorption less likely . Temperature , currents and the types of bacteria present could be other factors important in determining how much makes it into the atmosphere . <p> However , Dr Dlugokencky noted that evidence of vast , rapid releases of methane from the sea floor has not been seen in ice-core records , which now trace Earth history back about 800,000 years . <p> Image caption Methane can escape from hydrates on or under the sea bed , and bubble to the surface ; higher temperatures may exacerbate the problem <p> Research ships make regular cruises to the Arctic and have regularly found evidence of methane bubbling into the atmopshere . <p> But , said Professor @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ there is still a lot of basic science to be done . <p> " We do n't know the distribution of the methane hydrates - we do n't know how pervasive they are in the region , " he said . <p> " And in places like the shelf areas north of Siberia , where releases have been found , it 's not clear how much of this is coming from the sea bed and how much is brought into the sea by rivers . <p> " Also , we do n't know how much of the releases we see are down to anthropogenic warming in the last couple of centuries , and how much relates to the natural warming we 've seen since the end of the last Ice Age . @@3956102 <p> Has there ever been such a tragic misuse of medical research time and money than the millions poured into unnecessarily disproving the link between MMR and autism ? Has there ever been such an unnecessary blow to public health over such a non-issue ? <p> In all honesty , probably there has , so do n't bother giving me examples . But it was certainly tragic , and it was certainly unnecessary . I wrote at length about it last year , when Andrew Wakefield was struck off , so I wo n't go into it in any great detail here . But as we report today , the British Medical Journal has -- after a lengthy investigation by the frankly heroic Brian Deer , a Sunday Times journalist -- declared that the whole sorry business was a " deliberate fraud " on the part of Dr Wakefield -- see their report here and their accompanying editorial here . ( Hat-tip to Ben Goldacre , by the way . Apart from Rebecca 's story in our pages , this does n't seem to have got much attention @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ a huge surprise , and in a real sense it makes no difference . Dr Wakefield 's supporters will continue to see it all as a huge cover-up and the BMJ as a stooge for Big Pharma , because everyone 's a stooge for Big Pharma when it comes to MMR and autism . Grieving parents looking around for someone or something to blame for their child 's autism will still see a link between the vaccine and the condition , because one apparently happened shortly after the other , and that 's how human brains work . And in a way it does n't help , because the more we can blame Dr Wakefield for the MMR scare , the more the press can continue to pretend that they did n't play a role , and that they were scammed along with everyone else . In fact , systemic problems in the way that science and health stories are reported were as much of a problem as Dr Wakefield himself . Forgive my laziness , but I 'm going to reprint part of what I wrote back in May : @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . But he is far from alone ; the MMR vaccine scare reveals a fundamental problem in the media reporting of science stories . <p> Every single " quality " national newspaper reported on the Lancet article and its repercussions eventually ( even if only a few reported it the next day ) . For years , newspapers ( including this one ) reported any tiny case study which hinted at a link , while often ignoring huge meta-analyses and population studies that confirmed there was none . You can see why this happens -- " vaccine may cause autism ! " is a much better headline than " vaccine does n't cause autism ! " . But while it is understandable , it is not excusable . <p> Further , a media obsession with " balance " is to blame . " Balance " in this case means the giving of equal weight to two opposing opinions . This can work in political journalism , but when talking about medical and scientific issues it generally involves having one person who " agrees with the science " -- generally an expert @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ which depressingly often means a crackpot or a conspiracy theorist . These two views do not have equal weight in the same way that those of a Government minister and his shadow might while talking about education or the NHS . But it achieves a minimal degree of objectivity -- it is true that these two people said these things -- so this shortcut approach to science reporting is often used by time-pressured journalists in search of a story . It is dangerous and inadequate , but I do not have a ready answer for how we could improve the situation . <p> It 's not the fault of individual journalists -- it 's systemic , and as I say , I do n't know how to make things better . The reporting of science in the media was the topic of my PhD research ( unfinished , alas ) , and I could n't come up with answers then , so I definitely wo n't be able to in a 700-word blog post . But every time you read a cancer-cure story , a vaccine-causes-anything story , a climate change @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ it for its attempts at " balance " , and remember what happened , for years , with Andrew Wakefield , MMR , and autism. 
@@3957490 <h> Transylvania : could this ' lost in time ' land be the future of European agriculture ? <h> Transylvania has maintained traditional farming methods for hundreds of years . As it faces the twin threats of intensive agriculture and byzantine EU policies , its model of under-development is attracting the interest of policy makers <p> Forget Count Dracula . Deep in the heart of Transylvania , an altogether more mesmerising scene is playing itself out - a vision of what life must have been like in a medieval village . <p> There 's not a brightly coloured shop or advert in sight . Horse and carts clatter down the dirt track roads and cows wander freely . There are barely any cars . And behind the tall walls of each of the old Saxon houses is a self-contained ' courtyard farm ' complete with a wooden hay barn , livestock sheds and a small vegetable plot and fruit orchard . <p> In the distance are unfenced wildflower-rich grasslands and communal hay-meadows and beyond that , thick , old growth forests where bears , wolves and wild cats @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ one of the 150 or so well preserved Saxon villages and settlements of southern Transylvania that have remained almost unchanged for hundreds of years . The so-called Saxons were German colonists who immigrated to Transylvania in the 12th and 13th centuries and they were renowned for being hard working farmers . The smallholder lifestyle continues to flourish here today , with most villagers entirely self-sufficient . <p> The good life <p> A typical household keeps poultry , a couple of pigs , 10-20 sheep , along with 2 or 3 cows that graze on communal village pastures by day and are milked by hand in the morning and evening in the courtyards . Fruit and vegetables are eaten fresh from the garden or preserved in pickles or jams for the winter months . As well as growing and rearing their own , villagers also slaughter and butcher their own animals . Every chimney has a special chamber for hanging meat ( predominantly pork ) for smoking . Many households make their own wine from homegrown vines and brandy from plums . <p> This self-sufficient way of life is still deeply ingrained @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . Farming dictates the rhythm of life here , both daily and seasonally - during the summer months , for instance , most families are out in their hay-meadows with scythes and rakes making hay for their cattle and sheep . <p> ' Practically everyone is a farmer in rural Romania , ' says Nat Page , director of the charitable conservation foundation ADEPT . ' Ninety per cent of villagers have land outside the village and their courtyard farms . ' There are some shops but they 're very basic - you would n't even know they were shops and most people only buy things they ca n't make themselves , like cooking oil , cigarettes and beer . <p> The food produced is organic in practice , although it 's not certified because the costs of certification are too high . This low impact , ' High Nature Value ' farming allows nature to thrive . Wildflowers are abundant , and many of the mammals , birds , amphibians , reptiles , fish and insects present are rare or protected at national and international level , and have disappeared @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ its work in the T ? rnava Mare area , 85,000 hectares in the heart of the Saxon Villages area and has had the area certified as a Natura 2000 site which gives it a basic level of protection . <p> There are , however , threats facing the landscape and local communities . ' People are not being allowed to produce jams and pickles in their own kitchen because of excessive interpretation of EU law by the Romanian Food Standards Agency , ' says Page . ' They 're being driven out of business and lovely old bakeries are still being closed down . ' This , he stresses , is not what Brussels wants . ' Brussels preaches diversity , flexibility and cultural traditions but it 's up to the host country to implement it . ' <p> Another problem is that 70 per cent of farmers are over 50 years old . Christi Gherghiceanu , ADEPT project manager who grew up in the area says , ' people older than 50 are reluctant to leave their homes . They seem to be happy with their rural lifestyle . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ because of the lack of financial opportunities in the area . ' <p> The biggest threats to the landscape are intensive farming - artificial fertilisers would seriously damage or destroy the wildflower meadows and high stocking rates could lead to over-grazing - or abandonment of the land . <p> Farmers ' markets <p> ' Inevitably many of the smallest farms will disappear , ' says Nat Page . ' The average size of a farm here is 1.5 hectares . In five years time it will be three hectares . We ca n't say that everything is going to remain the same . But we can say we hope small- scale farming has a future in the area . ' <p> Founded in 2004 , ADEPT 's main objective is to protect the fragile biodiversity of Transylvania and use it to benefit local communities . ' We did n't just parade in with a load of money telling them what to do , ' explains Page . ' We employed local people and we 've helped small farmers in the real world . If you want to conserve an area but @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . ' <p> ADEPT , funded by Defra 's Darwin Initiative , Orange Romania and Innovation Norway , has assisted small farmers in two main ways . The first is to help them find a market by organising regular farmer 's markets in nearby towns and cities and enabling producers to get their kitchens authorised ( by persuading inspectors not to excessively interpret the Brussels guidance ) . ADEPT , based in the large Saxon village of Saschiz , also provides a modern ' food barn ' authorised by the Romanian Food Safety Authority where people can produce food . The 20 producers it works with now sell 70,000 euros worth of produce a year through markets , although ADEPT is keen to work with more . In addition , ADEPT has helped 65 small-scale farmers gain an income again by finding a market for milk . By working with them to improve hygiene and equipment , the farmers had their milk collection reinstated . <p> The second aspect of what ADEPT does is to help small-scale farmers get grants from the EU 's Common Agricultural Policy . Many farmers own @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 1 payments . ADEPT is dealing this at a policy level , promoting higher payments for small farmers . <p> To CAP it off <p> ADEPT has also helped farmers benefit from biodiversity conservation . As the area is a Natura 2000 and HNV landscape , farmers are eligible for CAP agri-environmental payments but these are not automatic - you have to opt for them . After running farm visits explaining the advantages of signing up , 75 per cent of eligible farmers within T ? rnava Mare have joined the scheme ; this is four times the rate of uptake in neighboring areas which demonstrates the desperate need for farm advisory services in such areas . <p> This will too , of course , benefit plants and animals . ADEPT 's botanist , Dr John Akeroyd , who often accompanies Prince Charles ( a regular visitor to Transylvania ) on his walks through this countryside says , ' the grasslands in this area are uniquely rich in Europe and appear to be still in good heart thanks to continuing traditional management by local farmers ' . <p> On a wider @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ an influence over the next phase of the CAP , 2013-2020 , in favour of High Nature Value landscapes elsewhere . ADEPT has been asked to present its results at EU meetings in Brussels because it shows a way forward to protect the small-scale farmed landscapes and communities across all of Europe . The Commissioners for Agriculture and the Environment both sent encouraging video messages to the High Nature Value Grassland Conference organised by ADEPT in Sibiu in September 2010 . <p> ' An amazing thing is happening within the EU , ' says Page . ' A few years ago everyone said these farms were irrelevant and policy favoured competitive farms . Now small-scale farms are seen as valuable for food and landscape , with massive benefits for flood and fire control , biodiversity and mitigation against climate change . They are increasingly appreciated as vital for Europe 's future . @@3957735 <h> How visual theatre can address deep issues <p> When Joseph Seelig founded the London International Mime Festival in 1977 , he was struck by substantial differences between works he was showcasing . <p> " Companies from England and France were doing pretty things and pretending to climb up and down stairs , " he recalls . " The work from eastern Europe was very different . It always had at least three different meanings . We had lots of work from Czechoslovakia . There were shows that you could see on many levels : often what you could see was a railing against tyranny . " <p> The shows from behind the Iron Curtain alerted Seelig to the capacity of visual theatre -- mime , circus , puppetry -- to do far more than just entertain and amaze . Seelig offers the example of a show by the great Czech artist Bolek Polivka : " It was the story of a man surviving a shipwreck . But really it was about a man surviving prison : it was about a man who had been in a labour @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ what it was about . <p> " When people think of mime , they think of Marcel Marceau , and that was n't really to do with any conveying of political ideas , " he adds . " But certainly during the bad old days in eastern Europe lots of ideas and meaning could be expressed far more eloquently without words . So silent theatre or visual theatre was then an important means of expression . But is n't it the case that 500 years ago it was the same in England ? There were legitimate theatres and then there was ' dumb show ' , which was often the more seditious form . " <p> Both the political and the theatrical landscape have transformed since the 1970s . With the collapse of the eastern bloc , the work changed , says Seelig . " Suddenly it all stopped , because there was no need for silent protest . " <p> So who has picked up the baton and what might they be talking about ? At this year 's Mime Festival two shows in particular catch the eye . And @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ do n't address tyranny or political shortcomings . Rather they focus on a more fundamental problem : the continuation of the species . <p> Both shows -- one French , the other American -- create a scenario in which the human race is under threat . It is a pressing theme and a common concern . But what can a wordless piece of theatre bring to the debate ? How can mime or circus discuss political and ideological issues ? After all , without language , it is hard to articulate a detailed argument on climate change or unstable international relations . <p> Seelig points out that both shows work , through mood , on an audience 's feelings , and that physical eloquence can communicate on a different level . Concepts such as fragility and vulnerability , for instance , can be expressed physically . The thrill of many circus acts lies in their inherent danger . In the hands of Mathurin Bolze , the French performer whose Du Goudron et des Plumes visits this year 's festival , the precarious predicament of the performers can acquire powerful metaphorical weight @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Of Mice and Men but developed its own narrative . A beautiful , enigmatic and unsettling work , it shows a group of five travellers trying to stay aboard a mobile set that hovers between earth and sky and pitches and rolls like a raft . We do n't know where they are travelling from or to but , forced to work together and cling to their little world to survive , they present both an archetypal image harking back to mythical voyages such as the Odyssey , and a metaphor for the problems facing humanity . <p> " I wanted to have the metaphor of an unstable floor , " explains Bolze in remarkably fluent English . " This object is like a small earth -- it 's a finished world -- you ca n't go out from this space . So this mobile stage helps us to show how humanity together can make things move . If we walk in the same direction the structure will balance more and more . And at the same time this balance can be a big danger for one human being . " @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ open to multiple interpretations . He adds that the shifting stage could represent the space of the mind . " You can be on a floor that is unstable , like when you search for ideas . " <p> But while this might sound rather abstract , the power of the show is that it is both enigmatic and grounded in very real physical action . It may demonstrate that people need to co-operate to survive but it does so tangibly . The artists are acrobats who dangle from the structure or leap on to one another . If the performers do n't work together , someone really will get hurt . <p> " If someone jumps you have to catch him , " says Bolze . " If you do n't , he falls . That 's a metaphor and it is also physical . That 's what circus offers . It 's a way to write questions in space . " <p> While Du Goudron et des Plumes presents an unstable world , Flesh and Blood &amp; Fish and Fowl goes further . Its creator , American artist Geoff @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ extinction " . Two office workers , marooned in the last office on earth , continue with mundane routines as the jungle encroaches . What begins as a satire on office politics becomes a much darker comedy . <p> " The origins of the project were really about human myopia and how we get so obsessed with our own petty concerns that we neglect the greater schema around us , " says Sobelle . " That relates to what 's happening ecologically but it 's larger than that : in a sense we 're contributing to our own obsolescence , working ourselves into a space we ca n't live in any longer . " <p> The show 's ecological concerns may not be new but its conclusions are striking . " It 's not a call to arms to reduce our carbon footprint , " Sobelle says . " The show is not supporting the idea that we can change . In fact , it is saying it 's too late . The world will keep on going -- it went on without the dinosaurs ... It 's not a hopeful @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , the show is , he insists , meant to be very funny . " It is a clown show and the figure of the clown is always the holy idiot . " He cites Chaplin and Keaton as inspirations and suggests that precisely observed physical comedy " has a unique ability to show behaviour " . <p> The absurd mismatch between the characters ' behaviour and their predicament produces the comedy but it also makes the larger point about human myopia . And in this the role of language -- or lack of language -- is significant . Sobelle studied at the Jacques Lecoq school in Paris and was inspired by Lecoq 's observation that we begin life without language . <p> " He was curious about the moment before language . And I think this show focuses on the moment before , but also the moment after . The characters are kind of becoming animals , you see them struggling to try to talk and have a normalcy but they 've lost their adeptness at language . When those constructs have fallen away we reveal ourselves . <p> " @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . " It does n't mean there is no place for language in our work but it 's treated in a very specific way . We 're looking for something outside of language . When somebody creates something in front of you without words , you as a spectator are able to understand it in a different way . It 's doing things to your brain that it 's difficult to express in words . Otherwise you would just do it in words . "

@@3958506 <h> As the Met Office desperately tries to salvage its reputation , another of this ' warm ' winter 's ice disasters is unfolding in the Sea of Okhotsk , writes Christopher Booker . <p> First it was a national joke . Then its professional failings became a national disaster . Now , the dishonesty of its attempts to fight off a barrage of criticism has become a real national scandal . I am talking yet again of that sad organisation the UK Met Office , as it now defends its bizarre record with claims as embarrassingly absurd as any which can ever have been made by highly-paid government officials . <p> Let us begin with last week 's astonishing claim that , far from failing to predict the coldest November and December since records began , the Met Office had secretly warned the Cabinet Office in October that Britain was facing an early and extremely cold winter . In what looked like a concerted effort at damage limitation , this was revealed by the BBC 's environmental correspondent , Roger Harrabin , a leading evangelist for man-made @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ reported by the blog Autonomous Mind -- still contains a chart it published in October , predicting that UK temperatures between December and February would be up to 2C warmer than average . <p> So if the Met Office told the Government in October the opposite of what it told the public , it seems to be admitting that its information was false and misleading . But we have no evidence of what it did tell the Government other than its own latest account . And on the model of the famous Cretan Paradox , how can we now trust that statement ? <p> Then we have the recent claim by the Met Office 's chief scientist , Professor Julia Slingo OBE , in an interview with Nature , that if her organisation 's forecasts have shortcomings , they could be remedied by giving it another ? 20 million a year for better computers . As she put it , " We keep saying we need four times the computing power . " <p> Yet it is only two years since the Met Office was boasting of the ? 33 million @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ had installed in Exeter . This , as Prof Slingo confirmed to the parliamentary inquiry into Climategate , is what provides the Met Office both with its weather forecasting and its projections of what the world 's climate will be like in 100 years ( relied on , in turn , by the UN 's Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change ) . Prof Slingo fails to recognise that the fatal flaw of her computer models is that they assume that the main forcing factor determining climate is the rise in CO2 levels . So giving her yet more money would only compound the errors her computers come up with . <h> Related Articles <p> In another interview , just before Christmas , when the whole country was grinding to a halt in ice and snow , Prof Slingo claimed that this was merely a local event , " very much confined to the UK and Western Europe " . Do these Met Office experts ever look beyond those computer models which tell them that 2010 was the second hottest year in history ? Only a few days after she made this @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of the worst snowstorms ever recorded . There have been similar freezing disasters in south China , Japan , central Russia and right round the northern hemisphere . <p> The only evidence the Met Office and its warmist allies can adduce to support their belief in the warmth of 2010 is that in certain parts of the world , such as Greenland , Baffin Island and the southern half of Hudson Bay , it was warmer than average . Yet even there temperatures are currently plummeting : Hudson Bay and Baffin Island are rapidly freezing , at well below zero . <p> The desperate attempt to establish 2010 as an outstandingly warm year also relies on increasingly questionable official data records , such as that run by Dr James Hansen , partly based on large areas of the world which have no weather stations ( more than 60 per cent of these have been lost since 1990 ) . The gaps are filled in by the guesswork of computer models , designed by people who have an interest in showing that the Earth is continuing to warm . <p> It is @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ depends on for its forecasts and the IPCC for its projections of climate a century ahead . And from them our politicians get their obsession with global warming , on which they base their schemes to spend hundreds of billions of pounds on a suicidal energy policy , centred on building tens of thousands of grotesquely expensive and useless windmills . <p> A vivid little reflection of how our whole official system has gone off the rails was the award in the New Year 's Honours List of a CBE , one rank lower than a knighthood , to Robert Napier , the climate activist and former head of the global warming pressure group WWF-UK , who is now the Met Office 's chairman . The more the once-respected Met Office gets lost in the greenie bubble into which it has been hijacked , the worse it becomes at doing the job for which we pay it nearly ? 200 million a year , and the more our Government showers it with cash and honours . <p> Meanwhile , in the real world , another weather-related disaster is unfolding in the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of Japan , where the BBC last week reported that a group of Russian " fishing trawlers " had got stuck in " 30 centimetres " ( a foot ) of ice . It did n't sound anything too serious . But , as my colleague Richard North has been reporting on his EU Referendum blog , the BBC underestimated the scale of what is happening by several orders of magnitude . <p> Although several smaller ships have now escaped , the two largest are still trapped in up to six feet ( two metres ) of ice -- including one of the world 's biggest factory ships , the 32,000-ton Sodruzhestvo . They still have more than 400 men on board . Three Russian ice-breakers , including two huge 14,000-tonners , are engaged in what looks like a forlorn bid to free them . A 14,000-ton ice-breaker can scarcely clear the way for a ship well over twice its size . And as the weather worsens , with gales , blizzards and visibility often reduced to zero , the chances of helicoptering the men to safety seem sadly remote . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the middle of winter , have allowed such a fleet of ships into a stretch of sea known as ' ' the factory of ice " . This is because all the rivers which empty into it from the Russian coast lower its salinity , making it prone to rapid freezing . But the Sea of Okhotsk has long been held out by the world 's warmists as an example , like the Arctic , of waters which , thanks to global warming , will soon be ice-free . <p> As we know from Prof Slingo , however , all this cold weather we are having at the moment is a local event , " very much confined to the UK and Western Europe " . Perhaps the Russian fishing fleet took the word of the Met Office , assuming that ice was a thing of the past . As the ice-breakers struggle to reach the hundreds of trapped men , and still-thickening ice threatens to start crushing the hulls of their ships , it seems that , short of a miracle like that which saved the Chilean miners , a @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ sad little nonentities in charge of our Met Office prattle on , extending their begging bowls -- and our politicians who have put them there remain smugly and inanely oblivious to anything happening out there in the real world . 
@@3958532 <h> The meaning of the universe ? It 's all in the punchline , by BEN MILLER <h> It 's one of the crucial lessons I learned from my years in the lab ; never try to anticipate whether a punchline might work in as much as you should n't apply meaning to an experiment before it 's finished <p> ' Science and comedy are about trying to discover a truth or staying true to oneself , ' said Ben Miller <p> Albert Einstein once said , ' Before God we are all equally wise -- and equally foolish . ' <p> Although I doubt very much he was referring to the inhabitants of the Cavendish Laboratory , Cambridge . With 29 Nobel prizes and counting , it 's quite difficult to find anyone equal to the task of taking them on scientifically . <p> This is the place where James Watson and Francis Crick determined the structure of DNA . Where Ernest Rutherford split the atom and earned himself a place alongside Sir Isaac Newton in Westminster Abbey . And I , Ben Miller , gave up @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Systems to run away and become a comedian . <p> Twenty years have now passed , and I think my parents are just about over it . Me ? I still have nightmares where my supervisor , the eminent Professor Sir Michael Pepper , wants to see my thesis and all I have to show is a pile of gags . Do you ever get over your first true love , I wonder ? I doubt it . <p> Happily , I do happen to believe there is something of a connection between the worlds of the comedian and the scientist . <p> For example , the famous Second Law of Thermodynamics tells us that everything in the universe is slowly collapsing into disorder . And that does n't just mean the Post Office . Put crudely , the concept is that rubble never rights itself -- that while man 's creations are ordered , nature as a whole tends towards chaos . A building can only collapse -- it can not restructure itself without our help . It 's this disorder that good comedians play on . And the best @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Einstein was the master of the one-liner - his quotes are endlessly recited by science boffins <p> I went back to Cambridge recently to make a rather grown-up documentary for the BBC Horizon team , and while I was sitting on the old wooden bench outside the Cavendish waiting for Professor Pepper , I felt like my young self who had n't done his physics homework . That somehow I was going to have to fake my way through a double lesson . It 's terrifying how quickly the mind jettisons information . <p> In reality ( as physicists are fond of saying ) , the professor was very relaxed all those years ago when I said I was going to throw it all in for a much more surface existence with a man called Alexander Armstrong . <p> That 's the thing about physicists . They spend their days contemplating the mysteries of the universe and how we might best use up the remaining 2.5 billion years left to the planet . One student running off to join the comedy circus tends to be fairly insignificant to them . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ sure that what I learned from Professor Pepper ever left me . The most important thing was a very scientific habit of understatement . The best comedy writing is understated , while men of science tend to be these huge 3D figures who operate in an incredibly calm and relaxed manner . Achievements are expected although , unlike comedy , not always to be applauded . <p> During my own research in those hallowed labs , for instance , I constructed a very small capacitor -- a device used to ' store ' electrical charge -- and in doing so was the first person in the UK to witness an effect called a Coulomb blockade , where the electrons in the tiny capacitor behave in a very unusual way . <p> On my recent return visit to the Cavendish I was walking down the corridor with Professor Pepper , and there it was , my tiny contribution , pinned up for the other postgraduates to see . It seems they still use it as a diagnostic tool today . I have left my thumb print on the world of semiconductor physics @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ stand-up audience . I wanted to jump up and down and shout , ' Look , look ! I may have left you for comedy but , still , I did do something . ' But at the Cavendish that sort of thing just is n't done . <p> Not that scientists are above jokes . In fact , they make them all the time . Einstein of , course , was the master of the one-liner -- his quotes are endlessly recited by science boffins the world over . Like him , the best scientists happily step outside of their complex arenas and hand down knowledge in an amusing manner . <p> Also like comics , scientists rise and fall on their original material . Both disciplines operate on an open stage and fakery is quickly outed . Nullius in verba -- ' take nobody 's word for it ' -- as they say at the Royal Society . Both disciplines are about trying to discover a truth or staying true to oneself . The method , I have discovered , is the same for both . There is an @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ empirical test for science . You know that if people do n't laugh , it 's not funny . <p> When I first performed in 1994 as one half of Armstrong and Miller , one critic wrote that we 'd ' invented a new kind of comedy , one that is n't funny and has no jokes whatsoever ' <p> Likewise , if an experiment does n't work , you go back to the drawing board . Both areas are about testing theories until they work . That is the scientific method . And it 's an important part of comedy as well . <p> When I first performed in 1994 as one half of Armstrong and Miller , one critic wrote that we 'd ' invented a new kind of comedy , one that is n't funny and has no jokes whatsoever ' . Clearly our initial experiment needed to be refined and re-tested . That 's the job of the comedian -- and why live audiences are as important a part of comedy as laboratories are to scientists . <p> It 's one of the crucial lessons I learned @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ anticipate whether a punchline might work in as much as you should n't apply meaning to an experiment before it 's finished . <p> On my return to Cambridge for my documentary , I re-learned how crucial this empiricism is to scientific thought . Scientists know for a fact that the average temperature of the planet has gone up by around one degree , or to be more precise , about three-quarters of a degree since 1850 . But how do we know for sure ? And did we measure it ? <p> It seems like such a simple question , but finding the answer for the documentary was a wonderful reminder of why I fell in love with science in the first place . It reinforced for me how crucial accurate measurement is . <p> Without measurement , the world is just a swirl of opinions and prejudices . When we lay those pre-conceived ideas aside and apply a tape measure , or a thermometer , we become scientists . <p> I have my own views about global warming , as I 'm sure you do , and they are @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ change . But my personal opinion is that the Intergovernmental Panel On Climate Change has it right ; that there is a 90 per cent chance that greenhouse gases are responsible for most of the temperature rises we have seen since the middle of the 20th century . <p> However , luckily , as far as I am concerned , so far we have seen few negative effects from this warming . There has been a small rise in sea level and some melting of the Arctic ice sheet , though the Earth 's climate is subject to a great deal of natural variability and only time will tell for sure if this is due to greenhouse gases or not . <p> As a scientist , I am not a doomsday merchant . Instead it will primarily be powered by @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . This will allow the ship to travel at speeds of 15 knots , fast enough to keep pace with conventionally powered industrial vessels . <p> Inside , the boat will have accommodation for 30 people and a secure communications room capable of streaming live footage from the boat to anywhere in the world -- even if the vessel is taken over . <p> She is due to be launched in June and will undergo sea trials for two months before going on a world tour in the autumn . <p> Stefanos Malandrakis , who is in charge of the building project , said it was more environmentally friendly to build from scratch rather than refitting an existing boat as Greenpeace had done in the past : " This is a boat which we are going to be able to use for at least the next 30 years , and it is an investment that is needed more than at any time right now because when you see the impact of climate change we still need to be in remote parts of the planet . " <p> Greenpeace boats have been @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , loggers , illegal fishers , GM food importers and nuclear testing . They are crewed by a mix of professional sailors and volunteers . <p> The first Rainbow Warrior was a trawler previously used by the Ministry of Agriculture , Fisheries and Food , built in 1955 and bought by Greenpeace in 1977 for ? 40,000 . <p> In 1985 she was in the Pacific , campaigning against nuclear testing , when she was sabotaged and sunk by the French secret service who attached two bombs to the hull of the ship . <p> A photographer on board was killed when he returned after the first explosion to try to retrieve his equipment . <p> Rainbow Warrior II came into service four years after the sinking of her predecessor , but is actually around the same age , having been converted from a deep sea fishing schooner built in 1957 . <p> Manuel Pinter , who was on the crew of Rainbow Warrior II between 1993 and 1997 , said that while it was cramped and uncomfortable , it had an almost magical quality . " When you have the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ place to be . Just by turning up in a place in the Rainbow Warrior it seemed like things could happen . Just the name Rainbow Warrior is a little bit magical . It will always be special . " <p> Plans are still being finalised for what will happen to Rainbow Warrior II when the new boat enters service . It will either by recycled , put in a maritime museum or be passed on to another operator . Mr Pinter is keen for it to become a hospital ship : " I would not like to see her disappear entirely . I still think she has a roll to play . " <p> An onboard satellite-communications system will provide broadband to allow the ship to stream live footage . A secure radio room and onboard server room sit behind steel bulkheads , meaning that even if the ship is boarded and occupied , it can continue to transmit . <p> The mast will be 11 metres ( 36ft ) wide and 50 metres ( 164ft ) high -- as tall as Nelson 's Column or a 15-storey building . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ from its crow 's nest . <p> The helicopter landing pad and storage bay will allow Greenpeace to film and monitor activities from the air . " <p> The graph published in the exam paper was titled ' Timeline of the mean world temperatures over the last million years ' , even though no such record exists . <p> Pupils were asked to mark with an X the " recent rapid rise in global temperatures " , as well as the coldest period . <p> AQA said the graph was simply meant to show " generalised trends " in global temperature and claimed that it displayed a " similar " pattern to the ice core reconstruction . <p> But Dr Benny Peiser , director of the Global Warming Policy Foundation @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ said the graph contained " shocking inaccuracies " . <p> " I have no idea where they have got their data from , but it 's completely wrong . It was taken from a highly regarded and widely used Geography textbook , Geography : An Integrated Approach . We took if from the 3rd Edition published in 2000 but the graph also appears in the 4th edition published in 2009 . We therefore expect that many teachers and candidates will be familiar with this graph . <p> " The ice core data is very detailed and would have had to have been simplified for the purposes of the question that we wished to ask . Therefore we used a graph readily available in the textbook above that showed similar general trends . "

@@3960697 <p> That 's because Robin Niblett does n't suffer from the usual pangs felt by thinktank bosses . If there 's one thing that unites this rare species , it 's the desperate desire to escape from the Westminster bubble and see the bigger picture . Niblett 's work focuses on the biggest picture of all . <p> Chatham House is the UK 's leading foreign policy thinktank . Ministers , experts and heads of state queue up to address its packed events . Behind closed doors , the UK government is assisted " at one remove " , as Niblett puts it , through the many and varied international contacts of its staff . In terms of international prestige , it 's up there with the top organisations of its kind around the world . <p> So it 's no surprise that , when I enter the director 's office , the surroundings are rather grand . Chatham House itself stands on the northern side of St James ' Square , just off Piccadilly . This is London 's clubland , the sort of place you 'd @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ You might as well come to hear an update about Gordon 's progress in Khartoum as you would about Petraeus ' in Afghanistan . The solemn tick-tock of a grandfather clock , the book-lined walls , the enormous desk - any Foreign Office ministers would be pleased with this . <p> But Niblett is n't an Edwardian . In fact he flatly rejects the popular notion that geopolitics has returned to the same sort of state it was at the beginning of the 20th century . His organising principle is about money , not politics . <p> " Those in charge depend upon delivering wealth and happiness in order to remain in power , " he explains . <p> " My sense is we 're not moving towards a single form of political organisation but we are moving towards a form of economic organisation where we still compete on the margins , but ultimately we 're all bought into the same game . " <p> It 's a neat little summary of the world we live in - the kind of thoughtful titbit which journalists gratefully seize on . You might @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and start stroking it . After all , he 's just made the sort of sweeping statement which would make most of us giddy with its breadth . Not Niblett , whose job it is to gaze at the world . <p> But that 's not true , is it ? Not at all . If Niblett 's purpose was solely focused on ivory towers and navel-gazing Chatham House would n't get far . Like any thinktank director , there is much more to the job than just pontificating about the state of the planet . So he spends up to 40% of his time , he estimates , keeping up good relationships with Chatham House 's funders . He has to oversee the work of ' heads of research ' , as he calls them , who are able to do media work , manage staff and projects and sell them to funders - as well as conceptualising ideas . " It 's quite a combination of managerial , intellectual and communication skills that are demanded not just of me but of every person in a senior position of research @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ but not always appropriate , on News 24 . Niblett 's assured charm is much closer to what the doctor ordered . <p> It 's a strange mix of big ideas and the more mundane tasks of keeping an organisation going . Both require one thing more than any other : a sense of strategy . Headlines about Afghanistan tend to suck journalists and politicians into a single-minded approach to the war against the Taliban . Niblett sees the bigger picture , arguing that the presence of British troops there actually has nothing to do with David Cameron 's global strategy at all . He 's interested in shifting attention towards China and India . " This government is worried that our ability to be best positioned to take advantage of it is going to be completely undermined by an overfocus on Afghanistan . " <p> Afghanistan is n't the big picture ? After a dizzy spell of my own , I realise that he 's probably right . <p> The big strength of Chatham House is in its connections with the rest of the world . Less policy talk , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ in the British foreign policy-making world by talking less about British foreign policy and more analytically about what we think 's happening outside , " he says . <p> Foreign Office officials can tap into Chatham House 's network of connections - with other governments , foreign businesses , individuals , similar organisations , etc - as well as its own expertise . " That accessibility , that knowledge , that insight , then becomes part of what they can use to develop their own policies . " There 's the big ideas , of course , but there 's also smaller-scale issues which - of course - are n't really that small-scale at all . <p> The " nitty-gritty " of how patents are developed and then protected is a big issue when it comes to the role technology can play in the future of energy security and climate change policy , for example . This affects the EU , the US government and the Chinese government . " We are working with the Chinese about how they 're thinking about climate change and energy security within a global context @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ achieve one of the UK objectives of UK foreign policy . " <p> Spending so much time looking at the world 's future , it 's no surprise Niblett is so ambitious when it comes to his own organisation 's plans . It 's been three years since he took on the job ; with Chatham House keen for him to stay on for longer , does he have any Stalinist five-year plan ? <p> Almost . Niblett is certainly expansionist ( this is more acceptable for thinktanks than it is for states ) . Chatham House may be on the same level as its US-based equivalents , like Brookings or the Center for Foreign Relations , but it has only a fraction of their cash . " I know we do n't have the resources at their disposal that they have , " he says . Resources equals money , and money equals people . <p> " My big objective in the next four or five years is to make sure we have the resource base - and that is funding - to be able to match them in terms @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ one more step up Chatham House could do . " <p> That 's the problem with dealing with the world : there 's always more you can do to make things better . It 's what Chatham House does best .

@@3963921 <h> ' Unethical ' flipper tags are damaging to penguins <p> Image caption Flipper banding can be useful if you need to find one penguin in a big colony ... <p> The standard way of tagging penguins for science - putting bands around their flippers - affects their survival and reproduction , a study has found . <p> French researchers , reporting their work in the journal Nature , found king penguins had 40% fewer chicks if they were banded , and lived shorter lives . <p> They say continuing to use the tags would in most situations be unethical . <p> Flipper bands have been used for decades to identify individual penguins so they can be tracked on land and sea . <p> They allow for easy visual identification of individual birds from a distance . <p> Some studies down the years had suggested they harmed the birds - for example , by creating extra drag when they swam , or by reflecting sunlight in a way that could attract predators . <p> But others had suggested there was no problem . <p> " There was a @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and you could find studies and some would say ' yes ' and some would say ' no ' , " said Claire Saraux from the University of Strasbourg and the French Centre National de la Recherche Scientifique ( CNRS ) . <p> " So our idea was to try to make sure - instead of doing one-year studies , to try to find out what 's going on over 10 years , " she told BBC News . <p> This study ... eclipses everything elseRory Wilson , Swansea University <p> The French researchers followed a group of 100 king penguins in a colony on Possession Island off the Antarctic coast . <p> Half were tagged with flipper bands , while the other half had tiny transponders implanted under their skin . <p> After a decade of observation , 18 of the transponder group were alive - but only 10 of the banded birds . <p> They arrived at the island for breeding significantly later in the Antarctic spring , needed to make longer foraging trips for food , and overall reared far fewer chicks . <p> " This study is @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ been done - it eclipses everything else , " said Rory Wilson from the UK 's Swansea University . <p> " It 's conclusive - it 's going to be very hard for anyone to argue against it . " <p> Professor Wilson , who was not involved in the current research project , tagged his first penguin more than 30 years ago . <h> Changing climate for research <p> In recent years , studying penguins around the Antarctic shores has been given a new impetus by concerns over impacts of climate change on the marine ecosytem . <p> Image caption The king penguin is the second largest species , and the deepest diving - in excess of 100m <p> In some parts of the region , krill - the tiny animals near the base of the food chain - are in decline , with potential effects on everything from birds to fish to marine mammals . <p> But in the light of this study , use of the principal penguin research tool may become unethical . <p> " I would say no it is not ethical , " said Ms @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ only on land , and that probably wo n't be a problem so long as you take them off the birds before they go to sea - and that could still be useful , because I can tell you that when you go into a colony of 50,000 penguins to find yours , it 's not easy . <p> " But there are a lot of groups that are still banding in sea-based research , and I 'm pretty sure it 's going to be controversial - some may want to continue with other species of penguin , but I 'm pretty sure the effect is going to be the same for other species too . " <p> The logic behind this conclusion is that if the bands increase drag in the water - which has been seen in captive Adelie penguins - that is something that should affect all species . <p> The Strasbourg group is one that has adopted implantable transponders as an alternative tool . <p> But unlike flipper bands , they can not be read from a distance - the birds must come into close proximity with @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . <p> In the meantime , scientists may have to go back to research performed using flipper bands and ask whether the results still stand , or whether they were distorted by the very tools used in the research . <p> " If you compared population trends in chinstrap penguins in different areas , say , and both groups were banded , and one does better under certain ice conditions , that would n't necessarily be invalidated , " said Rory Wilson . <p> " But if you 've tried to measure the mortality rate and say ' it 's down to over-fishing ' or something , and you have n't considered bands as contributing to mortality , then you 'd need to re-assess your data . @@3965592 <h> Why I want the Brit awards to remain a Hogarthian boxing ring of egos , boozed up celebrities and ostentation <p> The nominations for the Brit awards have just been announced . Robbie Williams has won the most awards ( 15 ) and he 's only got two good songs . And in the last few years , big winners have included Dido , Duffy and Kaiser Chiefs : hawkers of pedestrian and puerile pop . <p> The evening is often criticised for pandering to alumni of the BRIT school . Ex-pupils Adele , Amy Winehouse and Kate Nash have both won the Best Female Artist or Critic 's Choice awards in the last few years . A coincidence ? Unlikely .
